=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given sequences of length N: A = (A_1, A_2, \dots, A_N) and B = (B_1, B_2, \dots, B_N).
Let S be a subset of \lbrace1, 2, \dots, N\rbrace of size K.
Here, find the minimum possible value of the following expression:

\displaystyle \left(\max_{i \in S} A_i\right) \times \left(\sum_{i \in S} B_i\right).

You are given T test cases; solve each of them.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format. Here, \mathrm{case}_i denotes the i-th test case.
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\mathrm{case}_2
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_T

Each test case is given in the following format:
N K
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N
B_1 B_2 \dots B_N

Output

Print T lines. The i-th line should contain the answer for the i-th test case.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq K \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i, B_i \leq 10^6
- The sum of N over all test cases is at most 2 \times 10^5.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
3 2
3 7 6
9 2 4
5 3
6 4 1 5 9
8 6 5 1 7
10 6
61 95 61 57 69 49 46 47 14 43
39 79 48 92 90 76 30 16 30 94

Sample Output 1

42
60
14579

In the first test case, for S = \{2, 3\}, the value of the expression is 7 \times (2 + 4) = 42, which is the minimum.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Implementation details:
import sys, heapq

def solve():
    it = iter(data)
    T = next(it)
    out=[]
    for _ in range(T):
        N=next(it); K=next(it)
        A=[next(it) for _ in range(N)]
        B=[next(it) for _ in range(N)]
        pairs=sorted(zip(A,B))
        heap=[]
        s=0
        ans=10**30  # maybe 10**30 enough? Max product 1e6 * (2e5*1e6)=2e17, so 10**30 fine.
        i=0
        while i<N:
            a=pairs[i][0]
            while i<N and pairs[i][0]==a:
                b=pairs[i][1]
                if len(heap)<K:
                    heapq.heappush(heap, -b)
                    s += b
                elif b < -heap[0]:
                    s += b + heap[0]
                    heapq.heapreplace(heap, -b)
                i += 1
            if len(heap)==K:
                val = a*s
                if val < ans: ans=val
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
if __name__ == "__main__": solve()

Let's think about formal lower bound more rigorously:
Let g(M)=sum of K smallest B among items with A<=M. For any subset S with max A = M, S is contained in prefix P_M, so sum_B(S) >= g(M). Thus value = M*sum_B(S) >= M*g(M). Therefore answer >= min_{M} M*g(M) over M values with |P_M|>=K. Note if S max M, |P_M|>=K. Good.

Upper bound:
For any M with |P_M|>=K, let S_M be K items in P_M with smallest B. Let M'=max A in S_M. Then M' <= M. The objective for S_M is M'*g(M) <= M*g(M). Therefore answer <= objective(S_M) <= M*g(M). Taking min over M: answer <= min_M M*g(M). Combined equality. This proof doesn't require M'=M. Good.

Now, final code can be:
```python
import sys
from heapq import heappush, heapreplace

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    T = next(it)
    out = []
    INF = 10**30
    for _ in range(T):
        N = next(it); K = next(it)
        A = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]
        B = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]
        items = sorted(zip(A, B))
        heap = []
        s = 0
        ans = INF
        i = 0
        while i < N:
            a = items[i][0]
            while i < N and items[i][0] == a:
                b = items[i][1]
                if len(heap) < K:
                    heappush(heap, -b)
                    s += b
                elif b < -heap[0]:
                    s += b + heap[0]
                    heapreplace(heap, -b)
                i += 1
            if len(heap) == K:
                cur = a * s
                if cur < ans:
                    ans = cur
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```

Let's maybe refine code for speed:
```
import sys
from heapq import heappush, heapreplace

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0
    T = data[idx]; idx += 1
    out = []
    INF = 10**30
    for _ in range(T):
        N = data[idx]; K = data[idx+1]; idx += 2
        A = data[idx:idx+N]; idx += N
        items = [(a, data[idx+j]) for j, a in enumerate(A)]  # But data idx for B starts here
        idx += N
        items.sort()
        heap = []
        s = 0
        ans = INF
        i = 0
        while i < N:
            a = items[i][0]
            while i < N and items[i][0] == a:
                b = items[i][1]
                if len(heap) < K:
                    heappush(heap, -b)
                    s += b
                else:
                    mx = -heap[0]
                    if b < mx:
                        s += b - mx
                        heapreplace(heap, -b)
                i += 1
            if len(heap) == K:
                cur = a * s
                if cur < ans:
                    ans = cur
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
```

```
items = [(data[idx+j], data[idx+N+j]) for j in range(N)]
idx += 2*N
```
This avoids A list and B list. Since data list contains all ints, we can directly create items from data. This is more memory efficient and faster. Let's do that! We know after reading N,K, A values are data[idx:idx+N], B values are data[idx+N:idx+2N]. We can build items:
```
items = [(data[idx + j], data[idx + N + j]) for j in range(N)]
idx += 2 * N
items.sort()
```
This avoids A/B lists entirely. It creates tuples referencing ints in data. Good. This is clean. Let's use this in final code. Need be careful with idx update. For each test:

Items:

A=2,B=1
A=2,B=2

Try optimal S includes lower A high B and higher A low B, but g at max may be lower than sumB(S) because it chooses two higher A low B? Example:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=3,B=1
K=2. Answer6 (S={2,3}), max3, g(3)=2, equal. If optimal S={1,2}? answer202 not optimal.
It seems for optimal S with max M*, S must be among K smallest B in P_{M*}? Is that always? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

K=2. Answer6 (S={2,3}), max3, g(3)=2, equal. If optimal S={1,2}? answer202 not optimal.
It seems for optimal S with max M*, S must be among K smallest B in P_{M*}? Is that always? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

Suppose optimal subset max A = a but uses only some items with A=a; group processing's g(a) uses K smallest B among all A<=a, which may include items with A=a that are not in optimal, but sum <= optimal sum, so M*g(M) <= value(S)? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

Suppose optimal subset max A = a but uses only some items with A=a; group processing's g(a) uses K smallest B among all A<=a, which may include items with A=a that are not in optimal, but sum <= optimal sum, so M*g(M) <= value(S)? Wait we already reasoned M*g(M) = answer for optimal max. Let's re-evaluate with an example where optimal max a but g(a) uses items with A=a not in optimal? If g(a) < sumB(S), then a*g(a) < answer. But upper bound says answer <= a*g(a). Contradiction. Therefore g(a) must equal sumB(S) for optimal S? Is that always? Let's find a counterexample:
Items:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=2,B=100
K=2. Subsets:
{1,2a}: max2 sum101=202
{1,2b}: max2 sum200=400
{2a,2b}: max2 sum101=202 answer202.
Optimal S could be {1,2a}, max a=2, sumB=101. g(2)=K smallest among A<=2: B=100,1 sum101. Equal.
Try:
A=1,B=10
A=2,B=1
A=2,B=2
K=2. Subsets:
{1,2a}: max2 sum11=22
{1,2b}: max2 sum12=24
{2a,2b}: max2 sum3=6 answer6. Optimal max a=2, S={2a,2b}, sumB=3. g(2)=K smallest among A<=2: B=1,2 sum3. Equal.
Try optimal S includes lower A high B and higher A low B, but g at max may be lower than sumB(S) because it chooses two higher A low B? Example:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=3,B=1
K=2. Answer6 (S={2,3}), max3, g(3)=2, equal. If optimal S={1,2}? answer202 not optimal.
It seems for optimal S with max M*, S must be among K smallest B in P_{M*}? Is that always? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

Let's consider performance: T up to 2e5, `sys.stdout.write(str(ans) + '\n')` 2e5 calls. Python buffered stdout, each call is a write to a TextIOWrapper, might be okay but could be slower. We can use `sys.stdout.write` with a list of chunks? Hmm. Another option: accumulate in `out` but if memory limit 64 MB, output list 15 MB might be significant. We can reduce by using `io.StringIO` and write as we go; StringIO stores one big string buffer, similar memory but less per-string overhead? Actually StringIO stores a list of chunks or a single buffer; writing 2e5 small strings may have less overhead than list of strings? It may still store all. But we can flush periodically? Not needed. Now, let's think about if there is a hidden issue with "A_i" sorted ascending but if A values equal and group processing, we compute candidate after all same A. Suppose optimal subset max A = a but uses only some items with A=a; group processing's g(a) uses K smallest B among all A<=a, which may include items with A=a that are not in optimal, but sum <= optimal sum, so M*g(M) <= value(S)? Wait we already reasoned M*g(M) = answer for optimal max. Let's re-evaluate with an example where optimal max a but g(a) uses items with A=a not in optimal? If g(a) < sumB(S), then a*g(a) < answer. But upper bound says answer <= a*g(a). Contradiction. Therefore g(a) must equal sumB(S) for optimal S? Is that always? Let's find a counterexample:
Items:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=2,B=100
K=2. Subsets:
{1,2a}: max2 sum101=202
{1,2b}: max2 sum200=400
{2a,2b}: max2 sum101=202 answer202.
Optimal S could be {1,2a}, max a=2, sumB=101. g(2)=K smallest among A<=2: B=100,1 sum101. Equal.
Try:
A=1,B=10
A=2,B=1
A=2,B=2
K=2. Subsets:
{1,2a}: max2 sum11=22
{1,2b}: max2 sum12=24
{2a,2b}: max2 sum3=6 answer6. Optimal max a=2, S={2a,2b}, sumB=3. g(2)=K smallest among A<=2: B=1,2 sum3. Equal.
Try optimal S includes lower A high B and higher A low B, but g at max may be lower than sumB(S) because it chooses two higher A low B? Example:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=3,B=1
K=2. Answer6 (S={2,3}), max3, g(3)=2, equal. If optimal S={1,2}? answer202 not optimal.
It seems for optimal S with max M*, S must be among K smallest B in P_{M*}? Is that always? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

It seems for optimal S with max M*, S must be among K smallest B in P_{M*}? Is that always? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

Suppose optimal subset max A = a but uses only some items with A=a; group processing's g(a) uses K smallest B among all A<=a, which may include items with A=a that are not in optimal, but sum <= optimal sum, so M*g(M) <= value(S)? Wait we already reasoned M*g(M) = answer for optimal max. Let's re-evaluate with an example where optimal max a but g(a) uses items with A=a not in optimal? If g(a) < sumB(S), then a*g(a) < answer. But upper bound says answer <= a*g(a). Contradiction. Therefore g(a) must equal sumB(S) for optimal S? Is that always? Let's find a counterexample:
Items:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=2,B=100
K=2. Subsets:
{1,2a}: max2 sum101=202
{1,2b}: max2 sum200=400
{2a,2b}: max2 sum101=202 answer202.
Optimal S could be {1,2a}, max a=2, sumB=101. g(2)=K smallest among A<=2: B=100,1 sum101. Equal.
Try:
A=1,B=10
A=2,B=1
A=2,B=2
K=2. Subsets:
{1,2a}: max2 sum11=22
{1,2b}: max2 sum12=24
{2a,2b}: max2 sum3=6 answer6. Optimal max a=2, S={2a,2b}, sumB=3. g(2)=K smallest among A<=2: B=1,2 sum3. Equal.
Try optimal S includes lower A high B and higher A low B, but g at max may be lower than sumB(S) because it chooses two higher A low B? Example:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=3,B=1
K=2. Answer6 (S={2,3}), max3, g(3)=2, equal. If optimal S={1,2}? answer202 not optimal.
It seems for optimal S with max M*, S must be among K smallest B in P_{M*}? Is that always? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

A=1,B=10
A=2,B=1

Try optimal S includes lower A high B and higher A low B, but g at max may be lower than sumB(S) because it chooses two higher A low B? Example:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=3,B=1
K=2. Answer6 (S={2,3}), max3, g(3)=2, equal. If optimal S={1,2}? answer202 not optimal.
It seems for optimal S with max M*, S must be among K smallest B in P_{M*}? Is that always? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

It seems for optimal S with max M*, S must be among K smallest B in P_{M*}? Is that always? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

Suppose optimal subset max A = a but uses only some items with A=a; group processing's g(a) uses K smallest B among all A<=a, which may include items with A=a that are not in optimal, but sum <= optimal sum, so M*g(M) <= value(S)? Wait we already reasoned M*g(M) = answer for optimal max. Let's re-evaluate with an example where optimal max a but g(a) uses items with A=a not in optimal? If g(a) < sumB(S), then a*g(a) < answer. But upper bound says answer <= a*g(a). Contradiction. Therefore g(a) must equal sumB(S) for optimal S? Is that always? Let's find a counterexample:
Items:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=2,B=100

{1,2b}: max2 sum200=400
{2a,2b}: max2 sum101=202 answer202.

Try:
A=1,B=10
A=2,B=1
A=2,B=2
K=2. Subsets:
{1,2a}: max2 sum11=22
{1,2b}: max2 sum12=24
{2a,2b}: max2 sum3=6 answer6. Optimal max a=2, S={2a,2b}, sumB=3. g(2)=K smallest among A<=2: B=1,2 sum3. Equal.
Try optimal S includes lower A high B and higher A low B, but g at max may be lower than sumB(S) because it chooses two higher A low B? Example:
A=1,B=100
A=2,B=1
A=3,B=1
K=2. Answer6 (S={2,3}), max3, g(3)=2, equal. If optimal S={1,2}? answer202 not optimal.
It seems for optimal S with max M*, S must be among K smallest B in P_{M*}? Is that always? Since if there is a K-subset T in P_{M*} with smaller sumB, then maxA(T) <= M*, so value(T) <= M* sumB(T) < M* sumB(S) = answer, contradiction. Thus S is a K smallest B subset in P_{M*}. Therefore g(M*) = sumB(S). Nice. So threshold at optimal max exactly optimal. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
was too long and got cut off. Need final code only. I'll provide concise code.
```python
import sys
from heapq import heappush, heapreplace

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0
    T = data[idx]
    idx += 1
    out = []
    INF = 10**30

    for _ in range(T):
        N = data[idx]
        K = data[idx + 1]
        idx += 2

        items = [(data[idx + j], data[idx + N + j]) for j in range(N)]
        idx += 2 * N
        items.sort()

        heap = []
        s = 0
        ans = INF
        i = 0

        while i < N:
            a = items[i][0]
            while i < N and items[i][0] == a:
                b = items[i][1]
                if len(heap) < K:
                    heappush(heap, -b)
                    s += b
                else:
                    mx = -heap[0]
                    if b < mx:
                        s += b - mx
                        heapreplace(heap, -b)
                i += 1

            if len(heap) == K:
                cur = a * s
                if cur < ans:
                    ans = cur

        out.append(str(ans))

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
